(231)

where the second line follows since the trace is invariant under cyclic permutations (i.e.,
\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[ABC\right]=\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[BCA\right]=\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[CAB\right]
).
Swapping the order of the integrals yields

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\mathcal{N}_{1}\int_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}d\mu(\hat{\mathbf{A}})e^{NTr[\hat{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}]}\times\Gamma_{2},

(232)

where we define
\Gamma_{2}
as

\Gamma_{2}:=\Gamma_{2}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\int_{{\mathbf{S}}}d\mu({\mathbf{S}})e^{-Tr[{\mathbf{S}}\hat{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}]}.

To evaluate
\Gamma_{2}
, we will make several mathematically convenient approximations.
(These will yield an approximate expression which can be verified empirically.)
We first assume for the purpose of changing measure that the (data) columns of
{\mathbf{S}}
are
statistically independent, so that the measure
d\mu({\mathbf{S}})
factors into
N
Gaussian distributions

\displaystyle d\mu({\mathbf{S}})=\prod_{\mu=1}^{N}d\mu(\mathbf{s}_{\mu})=\prod_{\mu=1}^{N}d\mathbf{s}_{\mu},

(233)

where
\mathbf{s}_{\mu}
is an M-dimensional vector.
The singular values of
{\mathbf{S}}
are invariant to random permutations of the columns or rows,
so the resulting ESD does not change.
This is very different from permuting
{\mathbf{S}}
element-wise, which will make the resulting ESD Marchenko Pastur (MP).

Using Eqn.
233
,

\Gamma_{2}
reduces to a simple Gaussian integral, which can be evaluated as a product of
N
Gaussian integrals (over the
M\times M
matrix
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}=

\displaystyle\left[\int_{{\mathbf{s}}}d\mathbf{s}e^{-\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbf{s}\hat{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{s}^{\top}}\right]^{N}

(234)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{N}_{2}\;\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right)^{-1/2}\right]^{N},

(235)

where the normalization term
\mathcal{N}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{2}:=\left(\pi\sigma^{2}\right)^{M/2},

(236)

where
\sigma^{2}=\mathbf{s}^{\top}\mathbf{s}=1/M

For any square, non-singular matrix
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
,
\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right]=\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right)
, so
it follows from Eqn.
234
that

\displaystyle\ln\Gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=N\ln\mathcal{N}_{2}\left[(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right))^{-1/2}\right]

\displaystyle=N\ln\mathcal{N}_{2}-\tfrac{N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right],

(237)

so that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}=(\mathcal{N}_{2})^{N}e^{-\tfrac{N}{2}Tr[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}]}

(238)

Substituting
Eqn.
238

into Eqn.
232
,
we can write
\Gamma_{1}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=

\displaystyle C_{\Gamma_{1}}\int_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}d\mu(\hat{\mathbf{A}})e^{NTr[\hat{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}]}e^{-\tfrac{N}{2}Tr[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}]},

(239)

where

C_{\Gamma_{1}}:=\mathcal{N}_{1}e^{\mathcal{N}_{2}}.

(240)

We can now evaluate the integral in Eqn.
232
over the Lagrange multiplier
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
(i.e.,
\int_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}
).
If we call this
\Gamma_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})
,
then (following Tanaka
[
84
]
) we can define the
Rate Function

I(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\int_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}d\mu(\hat{\mathbf{A}})e^{-NI(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})},

(241)

where

\displaystyle I(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})=-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right].

(242)

We can formally evaluate the integral in Eqn.
241
in the large-
N
limit using a Saddle Point Approximation (SPA)
(see Section
4.2
, Eqn.
68
), as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\rightarrow\sqrt{\dfrac{(2\pi)^{N/2}}{N\|I\|}}e^{-NI^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})},

(243)

where
I^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})
is the maximum value over all
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
for fixed
{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}
, obtained using

\displaystyle I^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}):=\underset{N\gg 1}{\lim}I(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})=\underset{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}{\sup}\left[-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right]\right],

(244)

where at the SPA we have stationarity,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\hat{\mathbf{A}}}I(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})=

\displaystyle-{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}+\dfrac{1}{2\hat{\mathbf{A}}}=0

(245)

and the Hessian of
I
becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\hat{A}^{2}}I(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\hat{A}^{-1}\right)\otimes\left(\frac{1}{2}\hat{A}^{-1}\right)=-\frac{1}{8}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}

(246)

where
\otimes
is the Kronecker product. Using Eqn.
245
, we have that
1/8

{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}
is the saddle point Hessian of
I
w.r.t.
{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}
. Solving the SPA equation, we find that the prefactor (i.e. Hessian) is given as

\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(-\frac{1}{8}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right)=\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)^{M^{2}}\left(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right)\right)^{M}
.

Substituting for
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
into Rate Function (Eqn.
242
),
I
becomes

\displaystyle I^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})=-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{I}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right]

(247)

\displaystyle=-M+\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right].

In order for this result to be physically meaningful,
we need that if
I^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})
grows,
then it must grow slower than
N
, and,
more importantly, that
\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)
be non-zero.
Importantly, when
\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)=1
exactly, however, then
\Gamma_{1}
becomes a constant,
and this simplifies things considerably!

A.4.2
Casting the Generating Function
(\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}})
as an HCIZ Integral

In this section, we express the Generating Function
\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
,
given in Eqn.
118
(equivalently, in Eqn.
113
,)
as an HCIZ Integral,
as given in Eqn.
122
.

Inserting
I^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A})
from Eqn.
247
into
\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\ln\left[C_{\Gamma_{1}}e^{-NM}\int d\mu(\mathbf{A})e^{n\beta NTr[\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{T}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}{\mathbf{T}}]}e^{\tfrac{N}{2}\ln(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right))}\right]

(248)

\displaystyle=\ln C_{\Gamma_{1}}-NM+\ln\left[\int d\mu(\mathbf{A})e^{n\beta NTr[\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{T}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}{\mathbf{T}}]}e^{\tfrac{N}{2}\ln(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right))}\right].

So long as the second term
\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbb{I}_{M}\right]
does not depend on
N
,
it will vanish when we take the partial derivative of
\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
to obtain the
\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{NN}_{gen}
, in which case it is not important.
We can then simply write the Generating Function
\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
as in Eqn.
5.2.2
as:

\displaystyle\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}=\ln\left[\int d\mu({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})e^{n\beta NTr[\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{T}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}{\mathbf{T}}]}e^{\tfrac{N}{2}\ln(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right))}\right],

(249)

or, in Bra-Ket notation, as

\displaystyle\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}=\ln\left\langle e^{n\beta NTr[\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{T}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}{\mathbf{T}}]}e^{\tfrac{N}{2}\ln(\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}\right))}\right\rangle_{{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}}}.

(250)

A.5
MLP3 Model Details

The empirical MLP3 Model implements the assumptions described in Section
5
used the following
procedures:

A three-layer Multi-Layer Perceptron was trained for classification on the MNIST dataset
[
117
]
. The first Fully
Connected (FC) hidden layer has 300 units, the second FC hidden layer has 100 units, and the third FC layer has ten
units for classification, matching the ten digit classes of MNIST. Input images are grayscale, and were rescaled to the

[0,1]
range. Following the keras
[
130
]
defaults, the weights were initialized using the Glorot
Normal
[
131
]
method, and the biases were initialized to
0
. Each model was trained using Categorical Cross
Entropy as the loss function. The loss function was
summed
over each mini-batch, which is the default behavior for
Keras, rather than being
averaged
, which is the default for pytorch
[
132
]
.

Optimization was carried out by either Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD)
without momentum, or the Adam algorithm
[
133
]
. The Learning Rate (LR) was set to 0.01 for SGD, and 0.001
for Adam. The LR was held constant, i.e., there was no decay schedule. Each algorithm proceeded epoch by epoch until the
value of the loss function did not decrease by more than 0.0001 for three consecutive epochs. At each epoch,
the
WeightWatcher
tool was used to compute metrics for each layer. Loss values reported are the average loss per labeled example,
and not the summed loss over each minibatch. Training loss is averaged over all batches in the epoch, whereas test loss
is evaluated once at the end of the epoch.

In some experiments, only one layer was trained, while the others were left frozen. In other experiments all layers were
trained. Models were trained using a series of mini-batch sizes ranging from
1
to
32
. For each separate training
run, the models were re-initialized to the same starting random weights, all random seeds were reset, and deterministic
computations were used to train the models.

Separate notebooks are provided for keras and pytorch implementations of the experiments.

A.6
Tanaka’s Result

In this section, we will rederive the result by Tanaka
[
83
,
84
]
that we use in our main derivation,
and, importantly, explain how to address the missing Temperature term.
For completeness, we restate it here using the notation of the main text:

\lim_{N\gg 1}\frac{1}{N}\ln\underbrace{\left\langle\exp\left(\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}\mathbf{W}\right]\right)\right\rangle_{\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}}}_{\text{HCIZ Integral}}=\frac{n\beta}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda_{i})

(251)

where

\mathbf{W}
is the
N\times M
Teacher weight matrix,

\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{N}
is the
N\times N
Student (correlation) matrix,
but
\beta
is now the inverse-Temperature (because we are working with real matrices),
n
is the size of the training set,
and we have added the
(\tfrac{1}{2})
prefactor (which will be clear later).

\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
is a complex analytic function of the eigenvalues
\lambda
of (the Teacher Correlation matrix)
\mathbf{X}
,
whose functional form will depend on the structure of the limiting form of (the Student) ESD
\rho_{\mathbf{A}}^{\infty}(\lambda)
.
We may also write it as
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})
below.
We call it perhaps somewhat imprecisely a
Norm Generating Function
because the final results for the Layer Quality
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}
will take the form of a
Tail norm
in many cases.

To apply this result, we note that
while the term
\beta
is just a constant in
[
84
]

(
1
or
2
, depending on whether the random matrix is real or complex),
it is not actually inverse Temperature
\beta=\tfrac{1}{T}
in the original derivation.
Still, we seek a final result that is linear in
\beta=\tfrac{1}{T}
,
so that we can easily evaluate
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
in the high-T limit, i.e.

\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial n}\tfrac{1}{\beta}\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},N\gg 1}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\tfrac{1}{n}\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},N\gg 1}

(see
11
).
We can introduce the new term
n\beta
by
simply changing the scale of
\mathbf{A}_{N}
since the final result is a sum of R-transforms, which by definition
are linear, i.e.,
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(n\beta\lambda)=n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
, however, it is instructive
to rederive the final result, with
n\beta
explicitly included.

Notation.

We start by rewriting the Tanaka result, Eqn. (
251
),
in our notation for the expected value
\left\langle\cdots\right\rangle_{\mathbf{A}}
operator, as follows:

\tfrac{1}{2N}\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},N\gg 1}=\tfrac{1}{N}\lim_{N\gg 1}\ln\underbrace{\int d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{A}})\left[\exp\left(\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}\mathbf{W}\right]\right)\right]}_{\mbox{HCIZ Integral}}=n\beta\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda_{i}).

(252)

where we have added a
\tfrac{1}{2}
for technical convenience (to make the connection with the LDP, below).
If we denote the internal HCIZ integral as

\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}:=\int d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{A}})\left[\exp\left(\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}\mathbf{W}\right]\right)\right],

(253)

then it holds that

\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}:=\tfrac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{Z}^{IZ},

(254)

from which it follows that

\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},N\gg 1}:=\lim_{N\gg 1}\tfrac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}.

(255)

The SPA approximates the Partition Function
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
, which is now an HCIZ integral, by its peak value.
For this,
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
itself must either not explicitly depend on
N
and/or at least not grow faster than
N
.

The trick here is we can choose an R-transform of
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}

that is a simple analytic expression based on the observed
empirical spectral density (ESD) of the
\mathbf{X}
.
And this can readily be done for the ESDs for a wide range of layer weight matrices
observed in modern DNNs because their ESDs are Heavy-Tailed Power Law
[
63
]
.
We can then readily express the Quality
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}
of the Teacher layer in a simple functional form, (i.e an approximate Shatten Norm).

Importantly, the matrices
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}
must be well approximated
by low rank matrices since the derivation in Tanaka requires this. Fortunately,
this appears to be generally true for the layers in very well trained DNNs,
which is what allows us to apply this withing the
ECS
.
In fact, technically we need to integrate over
d\mu(\tilde{\mathbf{A}})
; this is straightforward
as this simply changes the lower bound on the integral from
0\rightarrow{\tilde{\lambda}_{min}}
,
both above and in the subsequent derivation.

Finally, we note that
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})
is kind of
Generalized Norm
because
it can be evaluated as a sum over a function of the
M
eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mu}
of the Teacher correlation matrix
\mathbf{X}=\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}
.

\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})
will turn out to be an expression similar to the Frobenius Norm or the
Shatten Norm of
\mathbf{X}
, depending on the functional form we choose to represent the
limiting form of the Student ESD,
\rho_{\mathbf{A}}^{\infty}(\lambda)
and the associated R-transform
R(z)
, and various approximations made thereafter,

A.6.1
Setup and Outline

To evaluate
252
,
we want to integrate over all Student Correlation matrices
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}

that “resemble” the Teacher Correlation matrix
\mathbf{X}
.
To formalize this idea,
we need to define the measure over “all desired”
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}
,
d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{A}})
,
in
terms of the actual
M
eigenvalues,
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{M}
, of the Teacher. And WLOG, the final result can be restricted to the
\tilde{M}
eigenvalues of the Effective Correlation Space (ECS).

Randomness assumption.

For real weights
\mathbf{W}
, we assume an
orthogonally invariant
ensemble,

d\mu(\mathbf{W})=d\mu(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{U}^{\top})
for all
\mathbf{U}\in O(M)
,
mirroring the isotropic Gaussian initialisation widely used in neural networks.
Crucially, Tanaka’s large-
N
analysis shows the resulting HCIZ exponent depends only on the eigenvalue spectrum, so the final integrated–
R
expression should remain applicable even when full rotational invariance is later broken in training.

Using a source matrix
\mathbf{D}
to represent
d\mu(\mathbf{A})
with
d\mu({\mathbf{W}})
.

We consider all matrices
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}
with the same limiting spectral density,
\rho_{\mathbf{A}}^{\infty}(\lambda)
,
as the limiting (
empirical
) ESD of the Teacher.
That is, we want
\rho_{\mathbf{A}}^{\infty}(\lambda)=\rho^{\infty}_{{\mathbf{W}}}(\lambda)
, where
{\mathbf{T}}={\mathbf{W}}
.
Of course, there are infinitely many weight matrices
\mathbf{W}
with the same
M
eigenvalues,
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{M}
, as the Teacher.
Let us specify these matrices with the measure
d\mu(\mathbf{W})
.
Doing this lets us then write the measure
d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{A}})
in terms of
d\mu(\mathbf{W})
as:

d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}):=e^{-\frac{n\beta}{2}Tr[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{W}^{\top}]}d\mu(\mathbf{W}),

(256)

where
\mathbf{D}
is some
M\times M
matrix, called the Source Matrix, to be specified below,
and the
\tfrac{1}{2}
here as well.
Indeed, the key idea here will be to define
\mathbf{D}
in such a way as to obtain the desired final result.
Notice also that we have added an
n\beta
term; this will be factored out later.

We can now represent the partition function

\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
, by inserting Eqn.
256
into Eqn.
253
.

\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
is now defined as an integral over all
possible (Teacher) weight matrices
\mathbf{W}

\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}=\int d\mu(\mathbf{W})\exp\left[\frac{n\beta}{2}\left(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{W}\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]\right)\right],

(257)

Observe that this integral only converges when all the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}
,

\left\{\vartheta_{\mu}\right\}_{\mu=1}^{M}
,
are larger than the maximum eigenvalue of
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}
, i.e., when
\vartheta_{\mu}>\lambda_{max}
, for
\mu\in[1,M]

(although below this will become
n\beta\vartheta_{\mu}>\lambda_{max}
).
Later, we will place
\mathbf{D}
in diagonal form, and we will obtain an explicit expression for its
M
eigenvalues in terms of the
M
non-zero eigenvalues of
\mathbf{X}
.
The eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}
will turn out to Lagrange Multipliers, needed later.

The Saddle Point Approximation (SPA) and the Large Deviation Principle (LDP).

To evaluate the large-
N
case of
\beta\mathbf{\Gamma}^{IZ}_{\bar{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
(see
254
,
255
),
we assume that the distribution of possible Teacher correlation matrices,

\mu(\mathbf{X})
, satisfies a
Large Deviation Principle (LDP)
.
An LDP applies to probability distributions that take an exponential form,
such that
\mu(\mathbf{X})=e^{-NI(\mathbf{X})}d\mu(\mathbf{X})
,
where
I(\mathbf{X})
is Entropy or Rate function
I(\mathbf{X})
.

In applying an LDP, we effectively restrict measure of student correlation matrices
\mathbf{\mathbf{A}}

to those most similar to the empirically observed Teacher correlation matrix
\mathbf{X}
.
We expect the measure over all Teacher correlation matrices
follows an LDP because the ESD is far from Gaussian,
the dominant generalizing components reside in the tail of the ESD,
and at finite-size the tail decays at worst as an exponentially
Truncated Power Law (TPL).

Three
3
steps to evaluate
\left\langle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{A}}
in the Wide Layer large-
N
approximation.

The goal is to start with Eqn.
257
and obtain two separate, equivalent
relations, Eqns.
258
and
260
:

1.

Obtaining an integral transform of
\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
.

First, we expand and reduce Eqn.
257
and evaluate the expected value of

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
in the Large-
\mathcal{N}\;
limit in
N
by expressing the
\rho_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)

for the
N\times N
matrix
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{N}=\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{S}}{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}

in the continuum representation, i.e., as ]

\rho^{emp}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
, to obtain:

\lim_{N\gg 1}\dfrac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=M\ln(\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta})-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int\ln(\delta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda.

(258)

This gives us an
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
in terms of an integral transform
\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
, which we can model.
48
48

48

This integral of
\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
is related to the
Shannon Transform
, an integral transform from information theory that is useful when analyzing the mutual information or the capacity of a communication channel
[
83
]
.

2.

Forming the Saddle Point Approximation (SPA).

We evaluate Eqn.
257
as the expected value of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

for the
M\times M
matrix
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{M}=\tfrac{1}{N}{\mathbf{S}}^{\top}{\mathbf{S}}

(but explicitly in terms of
d\mu(\mathbf{X})
).
Then, taking in the large-
N
approximation using the SPA,
(and which can be done implicitly using the LDP), we obtain

\lim_{N\gg 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]\simeq\int\exp\left(n\beta N\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]\right)d\mu(\mathbf{X})\approx\exp(n\beta N\mathcal{G}^{max})

(259)

where
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})
depends on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})
, and
\mathcal{G}^{max}=\sup_{\mathbf{X}}\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})
.
We can then write

\lim_{N\gg 1}\dfrac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]\approx n\beta\mathcal{G}^{max},

(260)

3.

Finding the Inverse Legendre Transform.

To do this, we now equate

\lim_{N\gg 1}\frac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\lim_{N\gg 1}\frac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

(261)

Then, we can form the
inverse Legendre transform which we will let us relate
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
in Eqn.
251
to the integrated R-transform of
\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
.

(See
A.6.4
.)

A.6.2
Step 1. Forming the Integral Transformation of ESD

(\rho_{\mathbf{A}}^{\infty}(\lambda))

We first establish Eqn.
258
, in Steps
1.1-1.4
.
This is done by changing variables under a Unitary transformation,
\mathbf{W}\rightarrow\mathbf{\check{W}}
,
evaluating the resulting functional determinant,
and then taking the continuum limit of the ESD

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
.

Step 1.1

To do so, let us first assume that Teacher correlation matrix
\mathbf{X}
and the source matrix
\mathbf{D}

are simultaneously diagonalizable
(i.e., their commutator is zero:
[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{D}]=0
).
In this case, we may write the generating function
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
in Eqn.
257
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{W})\exp\frac{n\beta}{2}\bigg(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]\bigg),

(262)

where we have defined

{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}=\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U},\;\;\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{V},

(263)

where
\mathbf{U}
(
N\times N
) and
\mathbf{V}
(
M\times M
) are Unitary matrices.
Since
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{I}
,
we can insert these identities into
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
in
262
, giving

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{W})\exp\frac{n\beta}{2}\times

(264)

\displaystyle\bigg(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{V})\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})\mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U})\right]\bigg).

We can identify the reduced weight matrix
\mathbf{\check{W}}
as

\mathbf{\check{W}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{\top},\;\;\mathbf{\check{W}}^{\top}=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{\top},

(265)

Rearranging parentheses, this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{W})\exp\frac{n\beta}{2}\times

(266)

\displaystyle\bigg(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{V}^{T}(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}^{T}\mathbf{U}^{T})\mathbf{\Lambda}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{T})\mathbf{V}\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{U}\right]\bigg).

We can now express
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
in terms of
\mathbf{\check{W}}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{W})\exp\frac{n\beta}{2}\bigg(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{\check{W}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\check{W}}\mathbf{V}\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{\check{W}}\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{\check{W}}^{\top}\mathbf{U}\right]\bigg).

(267)

Since the Trace operator
\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\cdot\right]
is invariant to Unitary (Orthogonal) transformations, we can
now remove the

\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
terms, giving the simplified expression
for our generating function
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
in terms of
the two diagonal matrices
\mathbf{\Lambda},\mathbf{\Delta}
,
the reduced weight matrix
\mathbf{\check{W}}
, and
the Jacobian
J(\mathbf{\check{W}})
transformation for
d\mu(\mathbf{W})\rightarrow d\mu(\mathbf{\check{W}})
, as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{\check{W}})J(\mathbf{\check{W}})\exp\frac{n\beta}{2}\bigg(\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\check{W}}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\check{W}}\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\check{W}}\mathbf{\Delta}\mathbf{\check{W}}^{\top}\right]\bigg).

(268)

Step 1.2

We can now evaluate the
integral using the standard relation for the functional determinant for infinite-dimensional Gaussian integrals
[
102
]

\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}=\left(\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}\right)^{NM/2}\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)^{-1/2}

(269)

where the Jacobian is unity for the Unitary transformation.

J(\mathbf{\check{W}})=1.

(270)

since
\mathbf{W}\mapsto\check{\mathbf{W}}
is an orthogonal transfomation.
We now use the standard Trace-Log-Determinant relation
[
102
]

\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\mathbf{M}\right]=\ln\det\mathbf{M}.

(271)

Let us insert
(\exp\ln)
on the R.H.S. of
269
, to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

\displaystyle=\exp\ln\bigg[\left(\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}\right)^{NM/2}\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)^{-1/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg[\left(\dfrac{NM}{2}\right)\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\dfrac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\ln\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg[\dfrac{NM}{2}\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\dfrac{1}{2}\ln\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\bigg].

(272)

Step 1.3

We now want to express
the generating function
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

in
272

in terms of an integral over the continuous limiting spectral density

\rho_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
of the correlation matrix
{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}
.

First, we express the Determinant of the matrix
\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}
in terms of discrete eigenvalues:

\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)^{-1/2}=\prod_{\mu=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda_{i}\right)^{-1/2}.

(273)

This gives the Log-Determinant in terms of the
M
(non-zero)
eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}
and
{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}
, as

\ln\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)^{-1/2}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ln\left(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda_{i}\right).

(274)

We can express the ESD,
\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
, of the
Student Correlation
matrix

\mathbf{A}_{N}
in terms of the Dirac delta-function,
\delta(x)
, as

\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i}).

(275)

Using this, the Expected Value of the Log-Determinant
in
274

can be expressed in terms of the ESD of

\mathbf{A}_{N}
as

\displaystyle\left\langle\ln\det\left(\mathbf{\Delta}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)^{-1/2}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta(\lambda-\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)N\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda).

(276)

Let us insert this back into our
expression for the generating function,

272
, giving

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
in terms of the ESD
\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}
as

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\exp\bigg\{\dfrac{N}{2}\big[M\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)\big]\bigg\}.

(277)

We can now replace
the sum over the
N
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
with an integral over the limiting
ESD,
\rho(\lambda)
, to obtain

\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda).

(278)

Observe that this effectively means that we are taking a Large-
\mathcal{N}\;
limit in
N
,
N\gg 1
.
This lets us write the Expected Value of the generating function
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

in
277

as

\lim_{N\gg 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\exp\bigg\{\dfrac{N}{2}\big[M\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)\big]\bigg\}

(279)

Step 1.4

Using the Self-Averaging Property,

\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]\simeq\left\langle\ln\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{A}_{M}},

(280)

It follows from Eqn.
279

that

\lim_{N\gg 1}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]\simeq\dfrac{NM}{2}\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\dfrac{N}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda).

(281)

The
N
-dependence now cancels out,
and we are left an approximate expression due to the remaining dependence of the continuum limiting density

\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
(for
\mathbf{A}={\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}
)

\lim_{N\gg 1}\dfrac{2}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=M\ln\dfrac{2\pi}{n\beta}-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int d\lambda\ln(\vartheta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda).

(282)

This completes the derivation of Eqn.
258
;
we have an expression for the expected value of
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}
,
evaluated in the Large-
\mathcal{N}\;
(continuum) limit in
N
.

A.6.3
Step 2: The Saddle Point Approximation (SPA): Explicitly forming the Large Deviation Principle (LDP)

We now evaluate
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
in Eqn.
259
as
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

to establish Eqn.
260
, .

Using the LDP (and following similar approaches in spin glass theory
[
96
]
),
below we will show that we can write the expected value of
\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}

in terms of
d\mu(\mathbf{X})
now (which is equivalent to
d\mu({\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny M}})
)
and in the large-
N
approximation, as

\lim_{N\gg 1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\int\exp\left(n\beta N\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})\right]-NI(\mathbf{X})+o(N)\right)d\mu(\mathbf{X})

(283)

where
I(\mathbf{X})
is Rate Function, defined below,
and
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})
is what we are eventually solving for.

Step 2.0

We start with the
expected
Partition Function

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

\displaystyle=\int d\mu(\mathbf{A})\int d\mu({\mathbf{W}})\exp\Bigl[\tfrac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[{\mathbf{W}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}\,{\mathbf{W}}\right]-\tfrac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{D}{\mathbf{W}}^{\top}\right]\Bigr].

(284)

The average over
{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}
affects only the first exponential; applying the SPA, we

define
a matrix function
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})
, depending solely on

\mathbf{X}=\frac{1}{N}{\mathbf{W}}^{\top}{\mathbf{W}}
, by

\displaystyle\int d\mu(\mathbf{A})\,\exp\Bigl[\tfrac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[{\mathbf{W}}^{\top}{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{\tiny N}}\,{\mathbf{W}}\right]\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl[\tfrac{n\beta N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]\Bigr].

(285)

which will be valid in the large-
N
in
N
approximation below.

We now note that given the duality of measures, we can assert

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{M}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}_{N}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}].

(286)

This lets us insert (
285
) into (
284
) and then write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

\displaystyle=\int d\mu({\mathbf{W}})\exp\Bigl[\tfrac{n\beta N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]-\tfrac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[{\mathbf{W}}\mathbf{D}{\mathbf{W}}^{\top}\right]\Bigr].

(287)

The now need to determine an explicit form for

\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})
. We introduce a new change of measure,

d\mu(\mathbf{W})\rightarrow d\mu(\mathbf{X})
.
Then, we show this lets us express
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
as
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{X}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
and to express it using the LDP.
Next, we apply a SPA to solve for
\mathcal{G}^{max}=\max\;\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}
.
Importantly, we also show how to incorporate the inverse-Temperature
n\beta
,
which is new.

Step 2.1

To define the transformation
d\mu(\mathbf{W})\rightarrow d\mu(\mathbf{X})
, where (recall)
\mathbf{X}=\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}
,
we use the (again) the integral representation of the Dirac delta-function
\delta(x)
:

\delta(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{i\hat{x}x}d\hat{x}.

(288)

This lets us express the transformation of measure
d\mu(\mathbf{W})\rightarrow d\mu(\mathbf{X})

(approximately) as

\displaystyle d\mu(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle:=\delta(\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[N\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\right])d\mu(\mathbf{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{i\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}(N\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W})\right]}d\mu(\mathbf{X})d\mu(\hat{X}),

(289)

where
\hat{X}
is a scalar (or really a matrix of scalars),
and we have

\frac{1}{2}

term for mathematical consistency below.

49
49

49

The full change of measure would require a delta function constraint
for each matrix element
X_{i,j}
, i.e.,

\delta\left(\frac{1}{2}N\left(X_{i,j}-[\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}]_{i,j}\right)\right)
.
Here, we assume the Trace constraint is sufficient for our level of rigor.

Step 2.2

Next, we take a Wick Rotation
50
50

50

The Wick rotation converts an oscillatory integral into an exponentially decaying one which should be well defined. Technically, this is an analytic continuation which needs to be checked, but following standard practice in physics we will assume the resulting integral is analytic and therefore well defined and we will proceed onward.
,

i\hat{\mathbf{X}}\rightarrow-\hat{\mathbf{X}}
, so that the terms under the integral are all real (not complex), giving:

\displaystyle d\mu(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{{Wick}}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}(N\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W})\right]}d\mu(\mathbf{X})d\mu(\hat{X}).

(290)

where
d\mu(\hat{X})
is a measure over

\frac{M(M-1)}{2}

Lagrange multipliers, and the normalization is

\mathcal{N}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{{Wick}}}=\left(\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}\right)^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\frac{M(M-1)}{2}$}}

(291)

Step 2.3

We now insert
290
into
287
, which lets us
express
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]
as an integral over the Teacher Correlation matrices.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{A}}[\mathbb{Z}^{IZ}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{{Wick}}}\int_{\mathbf{X}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}e^{N\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]+\frac{N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\mathbf{X}\right]}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\right]}e^{\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]}d\mu(\hat{X})d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{{Wick}}}\int_{\mathbf{X}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}e^{N\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]+\frac{N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\mathbf{X}\right]}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}\hat{X}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]+\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]}d\mu(\hat{X})d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{{Wick}}}\int_{\mathbf{X}}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}e^{N\frac{n\beta}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})\right]+\frac{N}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\hat{X}\mathbf{X}\right]}e^{\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{W}(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\hat{X})\mathbf{W}^{\top}\right]}d\mu(\hat{X})d\mu(\mathbf{\mathbf{X}}).

(292)

Step 2.4

We can now rearrange terms to make this expression look like the Eqn.
283

In Large Deviations Theory, the Rate Function is defined by the Legendre Transform,

\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{X})=\underset{\mathbf{\check{X}}}{\sup}\left[Tr\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{{X}}^{\top}\mathbf{\check{X}}-\ln\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})\right],

(293)

where
\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})
is the Moment Generating Function,

\ln\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})
, is the Cumulant Generating Function, and
and
\mathbf{\check{X}}
is a (matrix of)
Lagrange Multiplier
(s).
\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})
is defined in terms of the (unnormalized) density
p(\mathbf{x})
as

\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{\check{X}}\mathbf{x}\right),p(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}

(294)

which, in turn, is defined in terms of the source matrix
\mathbf{D}
,

p(\mathbf{x})=\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\top}n\beta\mathbf{D}\mathbf{x}\right).

(295)

The moment generating function
\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})
is then given by

\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})=\int\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{T}(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}})\mathbf{x}\right)d\mathbf{x}=(2\pi)^{\frac{M}{2}}\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(296)

Step 2.5

The Saddle Point Approximation (SPA) can be used to solve for
\mathcal{I}(\mathbf{\check{X}})

by solving for the stationary conditions

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{\check{X}}}I(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{\check{X}})=0.

(297)

First, let us compute
\ln\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})
as:

\ln\mathbb{M}(\mathbf{\check{X}})=\frac{M}{2}\ln(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}}\right).

(298)

Substituting this into the expression for the Legendre transform, we obtain:

I(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{\check{X}})=\sup_{\mathbf{\check{X}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}\mathbf{\check{X}}\right]-\frac{M}{2}\ln(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}}\right)\right].

(299)

The supremum of this expression is attained at the value of
\mathbf{\check{X}}
that satisfies:

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{\check{X}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}\mathbf{\check{X}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}}\right)\right]=0.

(300)

Taking the derivative, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{X}+\frac{1}{2}(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}})^{-1}=0,

(301)

which simplifies to:

\mathbf{X}=(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{\check{X}})^{-1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{\check{X}}=n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{X}^{-1}.

(302)

Substituting
\mathbf{\check{X}}=n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{X}^{-1}
back into the expression for
I(\mathbf{X})
, we obtain:

I(\mathbf{X})=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}(n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{X}^{-1})\right]-\frac{M}{2}\ln(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}^{-1}\right)\right].

(303)

\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}-\mathbf{I}\right]=\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}\right]-N,

(304)

\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}^{-1}\right)=-\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}\right),

(305)

we get:

I(\mathbf{X})=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}\right]-\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}\right)-M-M\ln(2\pi)\right].

(306)

Finally, we express
I(\mathbf{X})
in the form:

I(\mathbf{X})=\frac{1}{2}\left[-M(1+\ln(2\pi))+\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}\right]-\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}\right)\right].

(307)

Step 2.6

n\beta\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{M}(1+\ln 2\pi)+n\beta\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{X})\right]-\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}\right]+\ln\mbox{}{\text{det}}\left(\mathbf{X}\right).

(308)

We restrict our solution to those where
\mathbf{X}
and
n\beta\mathbf{D}
can be diagonalized simultaneously.
In particular, this lets us write

\mbox{}\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathbf{X}n\beta\mathbf{D}\right]=\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}n\beta\delta_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu},

(309)

where
n\beta\delta_{\mu}
and
\lambda_{\mu}
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{X}
and
n\beta\mathbf{D}
, resp.

We can now write the maximum value of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
\mathcal{G}^{max}
, as

n\beta\mathcal{G}^{max}=M\left(1+\ln\frac{2\pi}{n\beta}\right)-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\min_{n\beta\delta_{\mu}}\left[n\beta\delta_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}-n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda_{\mu})+\ln\lambda_{\mu}\right].

(310)

A.6.4
Expressing the Norm Generating Function

(\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda))
as the Integrated R-transform

(R(z))
of the Correlation Matrix
(\mathbf{A})

Having completed both steps, let us combine Eqns.
258
,
282

with
260
and
310
.
We follow the first arguments by Tanaka
[
83
]
(which follows Cherrier
[
134
]
).

\displaystyle M\ln(\dfrac{{2}\pi}{n\beta})-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int\ln(n\beta\delta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda=M\left(1+\ln\frac{2\pi}{n\beta}\right)-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\min_{n\beta\delta_{\mu}}\left[n\beta\delta_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}-n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda_{\mu})+\ln\lambda_{\mu}\right].

(311)

By canceling the
\ln\frac{2\pi}{n\beta}
term from both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\int\ln(n\beta\delta_{\mu}-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda=M-\sum_{\mu=1}^{M}\min_{n\beta\delta_{\mu}}\left[n\beta\delta_{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}-n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda_{\mu})+\ln\lambda_{\mu}\right].

(312)

Since this is true for every
\mu
, we can solve this for any arbitrary eigenvalue
\lambda_{\mu}
.

Dropping the
\mu
subscript, we have the following identity:

\displaystyle\min_{\delta}\left[n\beta\delta\lambda-n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)+\ln\lambda\right]=1-\int\ln(n\beta\delta-\lambda)\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda.

(313)

We need to invert
313
in order to find
n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
.
If we choose the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{D}
such that
n\beta\delta_{\mu}>\lambda_{max}
for all
\mu
, then
this relation is concave and therefore invertible via a Legendre transform.

This gives

n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=n\beta\delta(\lambda)\lambda-\int\ln[n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda]\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda-\ln\lambda-1,

(314)

where we need to define
n\beta\delta(\lambda)
, which (not to be confused with the Dirac delta-function), describes
the functional dependence between the eigenvalues of the source matrix
\mathbf{D}
and the Student Correlation Matrix
\mathbf{A}
.

\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)
is computed by minimizing over
\delta
, ensuring the relationship holds for the entire spectrum.
So let us take the derivative of
n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}
w.r.t.
\lambda
.
Term by term, this gives:

\dfrac{d}{d\lambda}n\beta\delta(\lambda)\lambda=n\beta\delta(\lambda)+\dfrac{dn\beta\delta(\lambda)}{d\lambda}\lambda

(315)

\dfrac{d}{d\lambda}\ln\lambda=\dfrac{1}{\lambda}

(316)

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{d\lambda}\int\ln[n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda]\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle=\int\dfrac{d}{d\lambda}\ln[n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda]\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)d\lambda

(317)

\displaystyle=\int\dfrac{dn\beta\delta(\lambda)}{d\lambda}\dfrac{\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)}{n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\dfrac{dn\beta\delta(\lambda)}{d\lambda}\int\dfrac{\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)}{n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda}d\lambda

We can now simplify by defining
\delta(\lambda)
implicitly by the integral relation

\lambda=\int\frac{\rho^{\infty}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)}{n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\lambda}d\lambda.

(318)

Combining terms, this gives

\frac{dn\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)}{d\lambda}=n\beta\delta(\lambda)-\frac{1}{\lambda},

(319)

Inverting the derivative, we obtain an integral equation for
n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)

n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}\left(n\beta\delta(z)-\frac{1}{z}\right)dz.

(320)

Notice since
n\beta\delta(\lambda)\approx\frac{1}{\lambda}
for
\lambda\ll 1
, then
as
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(0)=0
and we set the lower integrand to
0
(for now). Even though Tanaka’s original proof assumes an analytic continuation without branch cuts, a heavy-tailed spectrum merely shifts the lower limit of the
R
-transform integral, so the expression for
n\beta\mathcal{G}(\lambda)
continues to hold.

To further connect these to the R-transform
R_{\mathbf{A}}(z)
, we recall that the Cauchy-Stieltjes (or just Cauchy See
135
) transform
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{A}}(z)
is given by:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{A}}(z)=\int\frac{\rho_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)}{z-\lambda}d\lambda.

(321)

The relationship between the Cauchy transform and the R-transform is then expressed as:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{A}}\left(R_{\mathbf{A}}(z)+\frac{1}{z}\right)=z,

(322)

which implies:

n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}R_{\mathbf{A}}(z)dz.

(323)

WLOG, as mentioned earlier, we can replace the lower bound on
\lambda
from

0\rightarrow{\tilde{\lambda}_{min}}
to obtain

n\beta\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{A}}(\lambda)=\int_{{\tilde{\lambda}_{min}}}^{\lambda}\Re[R_{\tilde{\mathbf{A}}}(z])dz.

(324)

where
{\tilde{\lambda}_{min}}
corresponds to the start of the Effective Correlation Space (
ECS
),
and, notably, take the
Real
part of
R(z)
.
We take the Real part because the imaginary parts coming from the upper and lower lips of
the cut cancel.
51
51

51

Alternatively, one might try to replace
R(z)
by its modulus;
doing so breaks the Legendre relation
G^{\prime}=R
and spoils the
additivity of free cumulants.

A.7
Existence of the Free
R
–Transform for Power-Law Spectra

In this subsection, we examine when the R-transform
R(z)
does and does not exist
for power law tails.

A.7.1
Analyticity criterion

Recall the Cauchy–Stieltjes transform defined in Eq. (
135
) of
Sec.
5.4.1
,

G_{\mu}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\rho(\lambda)}{z-\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\operatorname{supp}\mu.

(325)

If
G_{\mu}(z)
is
holomorphic
at
z=\infty
one may invert the map

z=G_{\mu}(z)
in a neighborhood of
z=0
and define

R_{\mu}(z)=G_{\mu}^{-1}(z)-\frac{1}{z}.

(326)

Obstructions to the existence of
R_{\mu}
therefore coincide with
non-analytic terms in the Laurent series of
G_{\mu}
about
z=\infty
.
These can be easily removed, however, by considering that
\rho(\lambda)
is always strictly bounded from above by
\lambda_{max}
.

A.7.2
Model and notation

We first model a
bare
power-law tail regularized only by a hard
lower cut-off
\lambda_{0}>0
:

\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)=(\alpha-1)\,\lambda_{0}^{\alpha-1}\,\lambda^{-\alpha},\qquad\lambda\geq\lambda_{0},\qquad\alpha\in\{2,3,4\}.

(327)

Normalization is immediate:

\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\infty}\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=1.

(328)

The choice
\alpha\in\{2,3,4\}
mirrors the Heavy-Tailed exponents most
frequently observed in neural-network weight and Hessian spectra.

A.7.3
Stieltjes (Green’s) transform

For
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)

G_{\alpha}(z)=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)}{z-\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(329)

Carrying out the integration yields

G_{(2)}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{\lambda_{0}\,\ln\!\bigl(1-\frac{z}{\lambda_{0}}\bigr)}{z^{2}},

(330)

G_{(3)}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{2\lambda_{0}}{z^{2}}+\frac{2\lambda_{0}^{2}\,\ln\!\bigl(1-\frac{z}{\lambda_{0}}\bigr)}{z^{3}},

(331)

G_{(4)}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{3\lambda_{0}}{2\,z^{2}}+\frac{3\lambda_{0}^{2}}{z^{3}}+\frac{3\lambda_{0}^{3}\,\ln\!\bigl(1-\frac{z}{\lambda_{0}}\bigr)}{z^{4}}.

(332)

The logarithmic pieces carry the entire heavy-tail fingerprint; their
placement in the expansion dictates whether
R(z)
will be available.

A.7.4
Moments and free cumulants

Algebraic moments exist only up to order
\alpha-2
:

m_{k}=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\infty}\lambda^{k}\rho_{\alpha}(\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-k-1}\,\lambda_{0}^{k},\qquad k<\alpha-1.

(333)

Hence

m_{1}=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-2}\,\lambda_{0},\qquad m_{2}=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-3}\,\lambda_{0}^{2}\quad\alpha>3).

(334)

The first two free cumulants are
\kappa_{1}=m_{1}
and

\kappa_{2}=m_{2}-m_{1}^{2}
.

A.7.5
R-transform for the bare tail

Define
w=G_{\alpha}(z)
and solve locally for
R(z)
.
Using the finite cumulants one obtains

•

\alpha=2
:

the logarithm appears at order
z^{-2}
;
G(z)
is
not
analytic at
infinity and the inversion fails.

Conclusion: no
R
–transform. Truly
1/\lambda^{2}
tails are
outside the remit of free addition.

•

\alpha=3
:

R_{(3)}(z)=2\,\lambda_{0}\quad\text{constant}).

(335)

Only a zeroth-order free cumulant survives, but that is
enough

for free convolution.

•

\alpha=4
:

R_{(4)}(z)=\frac{3}{2}\,\lambda_{0}+\frac{3}{4}\,\lambda_{0}^{2}\,w.

(336)

Here the series truncates after the linear term; higher cumulants
diverge.

These three cases show explicitly how
incremental
changes in the
tail exponent alter the analytic status of
G(z)
and hence of
R(z)
. Since the higher-order free cumulants diverge, the R-transform cannot be a finite polynomial. It must either be an infinite series (where the terms beyond a certain point don’t vanish) or, more strongly, exhibit non-analytic behavior (like the logarithmic terms present in
G(z)
) because its Taylor series coefficients (the cumulants) become infinite. Fortunately,
R(z)
can be defined if we ensure that
\rho(\lambda)
has compact support.

A.7.6
Truncated
\alpha=2
power law

Introduce a cut-off
\lambda_{max}>\lambda_{0}
and set

\rho_{\mathrm{tr}}(\lambda)=C\,\lambda^{-2},\qquad\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{max},\qquad C=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{-1}-\lambda_{max}^{-1}}.

(337)

Exact integration gives

G_{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=C\Biggl[\frac{\log\lambda_{max}-\log(\lambda-z)-\log\lambda_{0}+\log\lambda_{0}-z)}{z^{2}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{max}\,z}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}\,z}\Biggr],

(338)

valid for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{max}]
.

Expansion at
z=\infty
.

Using

\log(\lambda-z)=\log z+\log\!\bigl(1-\lambda_{max}/z\bigr)
and

\log(\lambda_{0}-z)=\log z+\log\!\bigl(1-\lambda_{0}/z\bigr)
,
the two
\log z
terms cancel and we find the regular series

G_{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{m_{1}}{z^{2}}+\frac{m_{2}}{z^{3}}+\dots,\qquad z\to\infty,

(339)

with finite moments of all orders. Consequently

R_{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=G_{\mathrm{tr}}^{-1}(z)-\frac{1}{w}

(340)

is analytic for
|w|
small.

Interpretation.

A truncation at any physically reasonable
\lambda_{max}
—for instance the
largest finite eigenvalue observed in a data set—instantly restores full
analyticity at infinity.
From the point of view of free probability the system now behaves as if it
had
all
moments, even though the raw tail is still
1/\lambda^{2}

within
[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{max}]
.

A.7.7
Key points and implications for
SETOL

1.

Cutting the tail at
\lambda_{max}=\lambda^{ECS}_{max}

removes
the non-analytic

\log z/z^{2}
obstruction and turns the free-probability machinery back
on.

2.

Any model density
\rho(\lambda)
with compact support has
G(z)
analytic at

z=\infty
; hence its
R
–transform equals the usual free-cumulant
series and is available for algebraic manipulation.

3.

In all theoretical derivations and numerical experiments in

SETOL
we therefore
model empirical spectra as effectively as
truncated

power laws
(i.e with finite bounds, not necessarily exponentially truncated).
This choice is both empirically justified (no spectrum is truly
infinite) and mathematically essential: it guarantees that
R(z)

always
exists.

A.7.8
Explicit
R
–transforms for the truncated tail

We can also provide exact expressions for the leading terms (free cumulants) in
R(z)
for
alpha=2,3,4
.
The compact support
[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{max}]=[{\tilde{\lambda}_{min}},\lambda^{ECS}_{max}]
guarantees that every
algebraic moment (and free cumulant) is finite. Hence the Voiculescu series

R_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\kappa{{}_{n}}\,z^{\,n-1},\qquad|z|\text{ small},

(341)

converges for every
\alpha>1
.
Below we list the free cumulants
\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2}
and the resulting

(R(z)
for the three exponents most relevant to
SETOL
.
Higher cumulants follow from the recursion in
Eqs. (
333
)–(
334
) and
need not be written out.

—

\alpha=2
.

C_{2}=\frac{\lambda_{0}\lambda_{max}}{\lambda_{max}-\lambda_{0}},

(342)

\kappa_{1}=C_{2}\,\log\!\frac{\lambda_{max}}{\lambda_{0}},

(343)

\kappa_{2}=C_{2}\,\lambda_{max}-\lambda_{0})-\kappa_{1}^{2},

(344)

R_{(2)}^{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\,z+\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{2}\right).

(345)

\alpha=3
.

C_{3}=\frac{2\,\lambda_{0}^{2}\lambda_{max}^{2}}{\lambda_{max}^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2}},

(346)

\kappa_{1}=\frac{2\,\lambda_{max}\lambda_{0}}{\lambda_{max}+\lambda_{0}},

(347)

\kappa_{2}=C_{3}\,\log\!\frac{\lambda_{max}}{\lambda_{0}}-\kappa_{1}^{2},

(348)

R_{(3)}^{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\,z+\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{2}\right).

(349)

\alpha=4
.

C_{4}=\frac{3\,\lambda_{0}^{3}\lambda_{max}^{3}}{\lambda_{max}^{3}-\lambda_{0}^{3}},

(350)

\kappa_{1}=\frac{3\,\lambda_{max}\lambda_{0}\,(\lambda_{max}^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})}{2\,(\lambda_{max}^{3}-\lambda_{0}^{3})},

(351)

\kappa_{2}=C_{4}\,\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{max}}\right)-\kappa_{1}^{2},

(352)

R_{(4)}^{\mathrm{tr}}(z)=\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}\,z+\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{2}\right).

(353)

—

Interpretation.

\kappa_{1}
fixes the mean scale of the heavy tail;

\kappa_{2}
sets its leading spread.
Because both depend only on the empirical cut
-
offs

\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{max}
, the two–term truncation already delivers
an accurate
R
–transform for free
-
probability manipulations inside the

SETOL
framework.

A.8
The Inverse-MP (IMP) Model

In this section, we rederive the integral
G(\lambda)[IMP]
for the Inverse Marchenko-Pastur (IMP) model, focusing on the branch cut starting at
z=\kappa/2
and extending to infinity.
This branch cut corresponds to the support of the ESD in this region.
We will:

1.

Explain the presence of the branch cut and its implications.

2.

Show that
R(z)[IMP]
becomes complex along this branch cut because the term under the square root becomes negative.

3.

Perform the integral
G(\lambda)[IMP]
, showing all steps.

4.

Compute the
Real
part
\Re[G(\lambda)[IMP]]

A.8.1
The Branch Cut in the IMP Model

The R-transform for the IMP model is given by:

\displaystyle R(z)[IMP]=\frac{\kappa-\sqrt{\kappa(\kappa-2z)}}{z},

(354)

where
\kappa>0
is a parameter related to the dimensions of the random matrices under consideration.
The function
\sqrt{\kappa(\kappa-2z)}
introduces a branch point at
z=\kappa/2
because the argument of the square root becomes zero at this point:

\displaystyle\kappa-2z=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad z=\frac{\kappa}{2}.

(355)

For
z>\kappa/2
, the argument
\kappa-2z
becomes negative, and thus the square root becomes imaginary.
This leads to a branch cut starting at
z=\kappa/2
and extending to
z=\infty
along the real axis.
This branch cut affects the analyticity of
R(z)[IMP]
, and it must be carefully considered in the integral
G(\lambda)[IMP]
.

A.8.2
R(z)[IMP]
is Complex Along the Branch Cut

For
z>\kappa/2
, we have:

\displaystyle\kappa-2z<0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sqrt{\kappa(\kappa-2z)}=\sqrt{-\kappa(2z-\kappa)}=i\sqrt{\kappa(2z-\kappa)}.

(356)

Therefore,
R(z)[IMP]
becomes complex:

\displaystyle R(z)[IMP]=\frac{\kappa-i\sqrt{\kappa(2z-\kappa)}}{z}=\frac{\kappa}{z}-i\frac{\sqrt{\kappa(2z-\kappa)}}{z}.

(357)

This expression shows that
R(z)[IMP]
has both real and imaginary parts when
z>\kappa/2
.

A.8.3
Calculation of
G(\lambda)[IMP]

We aim to compute the integral:

\displaystyle G(\lambda)[IMP]:=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda}\Re[R(z)[IMP]],dz,

(358)

where
\lambda_{0}\geq\kappa/2
.

Where the Real part of
R(z)[IMP]
is

\displaystyle\text{Re}[R(z)[IMP]]=\frac{\kappa}{z}.

(359)

The integral of this is

\displaystyle\Re[G(\lambda)[IMP]]=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda}\frac{\kappa}{z},dz=\kappa\left[\ln z\right]_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda}=\kappa\left(\ln\lambda-\ln\lambda_{0}\right).

(360)
